She actually brought me one when I finally finished my master's degree, which was so sweet of her because we didn't always see eye to eye on the property line... \n\nBut anyway, yes, I just keep having those thoughts about not waking up, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up and feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose it’s been about eight months now. Yes, right around the time my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. It was such a shock, really. He used to be a structural engineer, you know. He worked on some of those massive suspension bridges out West. We took a road trip out there once, oh, it must have been the late nineties, and he actually made us pull over on the highway so he could explain the load-bearing cables to me. I never really had a head for physics, even with my own degree—mine is in art history—but he was just so passionate about it. \n\nHe bought this dreadful little souvenir snow globe at a gas station on that trip, and it sat on his desk for years until his cat knocked it over... What was the cat's name? Barnaby, I think. No, wait, Barnaby was the terrier he had before the cat. Such a loud dog. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, yes. Ever since things went downhill with him and he started acting so differently about eight months ago, I've just been feeling this way and having those thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like art history or any hobbies?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I used to love painting. Watercolors, mostly. I still set out my paints and brushes sometimes, on the little table by the window where the light is best. But I’ll just sit there staring at the blank paper, and eventually, I just put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. \n\nIt reminds me of this one time I went to an exhibit of J.M.W. Turner's work in London. Oh, the way he captured light! It was just breathtaking. I went with my friend, Beatrice. She wore this enormous, ridiculous hat the whole time we were in the gallery, and I kept having to lean around her just to see the paintings. We had high tea afterward at this lovely little place near the museum. They had these tiny cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off, and Beatrice insisted on asking the waiter for the recipe for their scones. The poor boy looked so confused, I don't think he even knew what a scone was made of, let alone the specific recipe they used. He had this little mustache that looked like it was drawn on with a pencil... \n\nBut yes, I just don't have the heart for painting right now. It's just too much effort for no joy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, over the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I would say that. It’s like... most days I feel as if a gray film has just settled over absolutely everything. Even when I’m doing something pleasant, like making a cup of tea or looking out at my garden. \n\nI have these lovely heirloom tomatoes in the garden this year, or at least I tried to. The squirrels are absolutely ruthless. Just last week, I saw one sitting right on the fence post, taking one single bite out of a perfectly ripe tomato and then just dropping it on the grass! The audacity of it. It reminded me of my nephew, Thomas, when he was a toddler. He would take one bite out of every single apple in the fruit bowl and just put them back. His mother—my sister-in-law, Margaret—she was a very tense woman, always ironing everything. She even ironed her dish towels... I never understood that. Who irons a dish towel? \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, yes, my mood. Even when I'm just looking at the garden or making my tea, there’s this quiet heaviness in me. It's there most of the day, nearly every day, and I just can’t quite reason my way out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, as if you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe so. I haven't felt particularly restless, and I don't think I'm moving any slower than usual. I've always been a fairly still person, really. \n\nNot like my friend Arthur. Oh, Arthur could never sit still for the life of him. He was always tapping his foot or fiddling with his watch. He had this magnificent vintage pocket watch, a gold one that belonged to his grandfather, I believe. He used to take it out every five minutes just to check the time, even if we were just sitting on the porch doing absolutely nothing. We used to sit out there in the evenings and watch the neighborhood kids ride their bicycles. There was this one little girl who had streamers on her handlebars—bright pink ones—and she would ride up and down the street until it was practically pitch black out. I always wondered how her parents got her to come inside for dinner. My mother used to just ring a heavy brass bell out the back door... you could hear it from three streets over. It had a wooden handle that was completely worn smooth from all the years she used it. \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with myself. Just my normal pace, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think my appetite has changed much, and my clothes all seem to fit the same. I still make myself my usual meals. \n\nAlthough, speaking of meals, I did try to make a beef bourguignon last Sunday. I found this old recipe card in my mother’s recipe box. It was written in her beautiful, looping cursive—she always used a fountain pen, even for grocery lists. She had this lovely Parker pen with a gold nib. It was a gift from my father on their tenth anniversary, I think. Or maybe their fifteenth? No, it must have been the tenth, because that was the year they went to Paris. My mother always talked about the croissants in Paris. She said they were so flaky that you’d end up wearing half of it on your blouse. She bought this beautiful silk scarf while she was there, a deep emerald green with little gold fleur-de-lis on it. She wore it all the time, even when she was just gardening. She said it made her feel elegant while pulling weeds. \n\nBut, oh, the beef bourguignon... I don't know what I did wrong, but the meat came out terribly tough. I ended up giving most of it to the neighbor's dog. He's a golden retriever named Buster, such a sweet thing, but he'll eat absolutely anything. \n\nSo, yes, my appetite is about the same. I just haven't been enjoying the food very much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fatigued or lacking in energy lately, even if you're not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I wouldn't say I feel particularly fatigued or tired in that way. I mean, I still get up at my usual time and go about my day. I have about the same amount of energy I usually do. \n\nThough, speaking of energy, you should see my niece's little boy, Leo. Goodness, that child is like a wind-up toy that never runs down. They came to visit last month, and he spent the entire afternoon running laps around my living room. He was wearing these little light-up sneakers—do you know the ones? Every time he stomped his foot, the heels would flash red and blue. It reminded me of those old disco floors from the seventies. My husband, Richard, and I went to a disco once, just to see what all the fuss was about. Richard wore this terrible polyester suit... it was a sort of mustard yellow color. I have no idea why he bought it. He said the salesman told him it brought out his eyes, but Richard had brown eyes, so I really don't see the connection. The salesman was probably just trying to get rid of it. We danced to exactly one song, and then Richard said the flashing lights were giving him a migraine, so we went and got milkshakes instead. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, energy. Yes, no, I don't feel unusually fatigued. Just my normal self in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re a burden to others, or perhaps feeling like you’re not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I... I have been feeling terribly guilty lately. It's about my brother. I just keep going back over his decline in my head, over and over again, and I keep telling myself that if I had just been a better sister, or more attentive, I would have noticed something was wrong sooner. I could have prevented some of this, you know? \n\nIt's just like when we were kids. He used to build these incredible model airplanes out of balsa wood. He had this whole setup in the basement with the special glue—it smelled awful, that glue, like acetone and something sweet all mixed together. Our mother hated it. She used to open all the basement windows, even in the dead of winter, just to air the house out. She had these heavy wool cardigans she would wear down there, the kind with the big tortoiseshell buttons. One of those buttons fell off once, and I found it years later behind the washing machine. It was a Maytag, one of those old sturdy ones that sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. My husband tried to fix it once, but he ended up with grease all over his favorite pair of khakis. I told him to just call the repairman, but you know how men can be about fixing things themselves. He insisted he just needed a different wrench. \n\nBut... yes, I just feel like I’m carrying all this guilt about my brother, and it’s very heavy. I just feel like I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still have some interest, even if it doesn’t bring you the same pleasure as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I still have a little bit of interest, but... it's just not the same. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes. I have this lovely little wooden box that holds all my tubes of paint. I bought it at an art supply store in London years ago. Oh, that store was marvelous, it smelled like linseed oil and old paper. The man who worked there had the most magnificent white mustache, curled up at the ends like a character from a Dickens novel. He recommended this particular brand of sable brushes to me. They were frightfully expensive, but he said if I took care of them, they'd last a lifetime. And they have! I always wash them with a special soap, it comes in a little tin and has a very faint scent of lavender. My grandmother used to grow lavender by her back steps. In the summer, the bees would just swarm those bushes. We used to sit on the porch and drink iced tea, watching them. She made the best iced tea, she always put a sprig of fresh mint in it. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I'll set everything out, the paints, the water jar, those good brushes... and I'll just sit there staring at the blank paper. And then I end up putting it all away again, because the pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's like the spark is gone.\n\nDoctor: When you set out your watercolor paints and brushes, do you actually find yourself wanting to paint, or is it more of a habit or something you feel you should do?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I suppose I do want to paint when I get them out. Or at least, I remember wanting to. I look at the little tubes and I think, \"Yes, today I'll paint the garden.\" We have these wonderful hydrangeas in the back, the big mophead kind. They're a brilliant blue this year because Richard put some sort of special acidifier in the soil. He read about it in one of his gardening magazines. He has stacks of them in the garage, right next to his workbench. I keep telling him he needs to sort through them, they're just gathering dust and probably spiders. I am terrified of spiders, ever since I was a little girl and found a huge wolf spider in my rainboot. It was one of those yellow rubber boots, the kind with the little buckles at the top. I screamed so loud the neighbors thought someone was being murdered. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the watercolors. Yes, I want to paint, I really do. I set it all out, the brushes and the water jar, but then... I just sit there staring at the paper. The urge is there for a moment, but then it just evaporates. I end up putting it all away because that feeling... that joy I used to get from mixing the colors and watching them bleed into the paper... it just doesn't arrive. It's just blank paper and me, and I don't feel anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like painting or gardening, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still bring you some enjoyment, even if it's less than before?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's like I was saying before, I suppose. It's not that I don't try. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, you know? I get out the little wooden box and the sable brushes, and I sit there. But I just end up staring at the paper. \n\nIt reminds me of the paper we used to use in elementary school, that thick, rough construction paper. We used to make these little hand-turkeys for Thanksgiving. My daughter made one for me when she was in first grade, and I kept it on the refrigerator for years. It was held up by this magnet shaped like a little teapot. We bought that magnet on a trip to Cornwall. Oh, the coast there is just breathtaking. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a thatched roof. The owner, Mrs. Higgins, made the most incredible scones. Real clotted cream, not that stuff you get in the jars here. She had a border collie named Pip who would herd the chickens in the yard. I always wanted a dog like that, but Richard said they need too much exercise, and with his bad knee... he twisted it playing tennis back in the eighties, you know, and it's never been the same. \n\nBut... yes, the painting. I just sit there staring at it, and then I put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's just entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not worth much or that you have let others down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I... yes, I suppose I have been feeling rather awful about myself lately. It's my brother, you see. He's been declining so much recently, and I just keep going back over it all in my head. I tell myself that if I had just been a better sister, or if I had paid more attention, I would have noticed the signs sooner and maybe prevented some of this. \n\nWe used to be so close when we were younger. We grew up in this wonderful old Victorian house with a wrap-around porch. In the summers, we would catch fireflies in glass jars. My mother would always poke holes in the lids with an ice pick so they could breathe. She had this old wooden handled ice pick that belonged to her mother. It was kept in the top drawer of the kitchen cabinet, right next to the rolling pin. That rolling pin was solid marble, incredibly heavy. She used it to make the most perfect pie crusts. Cherry pie was my brother's absolute favorite. We had a cherry tree in the backyard, but the birds usually got to them before we could. \n\nOh, those birds... there was a cardinal that used to tap on the kitchen window every morning. I think he saw his reflection and thought it was another bird. My husband, Richard, he tried to put up some of those shiny ribbons to scare it away, but it never worked. Richard is always trying to fix things around the house, but he usually just ends up making a bigger mess. Last week he tried to fix a leaky faucet in the guest bathroom and ended up flooding the floor. We had to use every towel in the house to soak it up. \n\nBut, oh dear, what was I saying? Right, my brother. Yes, I just feel this terrible weight, this guilt, like I failed him by not noticing how bad things were getting. I just feel like I've let him down completely.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in all activities you used to enjoy, or is there still at least one thing that you take some interest in, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I think I've lost interest in just about everything, honestly. I know I mentioned the painting before. I still try, you know. I get the watercolor things out, the little wooden box and the sable brushes. I set it all up. But then I just sit there staring at the blank paper. \n\nPaper is so expensive these days, isn't it? I remember when you could buy a whole pad of good quality watercolor paper for just a few dollars at the art supply store downtown. It was a lovely little shop, run by a man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore a bowtie, even in the middle of summer. He had the most magnificent handlebar mustache, just like a character out of a Dickens novel. I always meant to read more Dickens, but the books are just so thick. My daughter reads them on her little electronic tablet thing. I don't understand how she can look at that screen for so long. It hurts my eyes just thinking about it. I need a proper book, with real pages you can turn. The smell of old paper, there's nothing quite like it. It reminds me of the library at my university. I spent hours in there when I was working on my master's degree. The oak tables were so solid, you felt like you were part of history just sitting there. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? The painting, yes. I set the paper out, but I just end up putting it all away again. That feeling, the pleasure I used to get from the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. I don't think there's anything left that I really care to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, or is there still at least one thing, even something small, that you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I know I keep saying the same thing, but it really is true. There's just nothing. Not even the painting. I still get my watercolor things out, you know, the wooden box and those nice sable brushes. I sit there with the paper in front of me, but I just end up staring at it and packing it all back up. The pleasure I used to feel in the colors and the light... it just doesn't arrive. \n\nIt makes me think of the light in our old sunroom. We had these huge windows that faced east, and the morning sun would just pour in. It was so warm, even in the middle of winter. My cat, Barnaby, used to sleep on the rug right in that square of sunlight. He was an orange tabby, such a sweet boy. He lived to be almost eighteen. We buried him out by the rose bushes. The roses haven't been doing well lately, though. I think it's the aphids. Richard bought some spray for them, but he left it in the garage and it froze over the winter. The plastic bottle cracked right down the side. It leaked all over his golf shoes. He was so upset about those shoes. They were a gift from his brother, the one who lives in Florida. Florida is just too humid for me, I don't know how people stand it. The air feels like a wet blanket. \n\nBut, oh... yes, the pleasure. It's just... it's just not there anymore, for anything. Not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, not even small things like a warm cup of tea, a favorite song, or seeing a loved one?\n\nPatient: I... yes, I know I sound like a broken record, Doctor, but it really is the truth. Even the small things. I keep going back to my watercolors because that was always my greatest joy. I still set my things out sometimes, hoping the feeling will come back. But I just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually, I just put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for that, not for anything. \n\nYou mentioned tea, and I actually did try making a cup of Earl Grey just yesterday. I have this lovely porcelain teapot my daughter gave me for Mother's Day. It has little hand-painted violets all over it. Violets are February's birth flower, which is funny because my mother was born in February. On Valentine's Day, actually! She used to complain terribly that my father would only buy her one gift to cover both her birthday and the holiday. He was incredibly frugal, my father. He worked at the bank downtown for forty years and brought his lunch every single day in a brown paper bag. Well, not the exact same bag, obviously, but he would fold it up and reuse it until the corners tore. He said Tupperware was a waste of money. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, the tea. I poured a cup from the violet teapot, but it just tasted like hot water. So no, I really don't find pleasure in anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore—not even things you used to find meaningful or deeply enjoyable, like painting or spending time in your garden?\n\nPatient: I... well, you mentioned the garden. I haven't even been out there in weeks. The weeds are probably completely taking over the tomatoes by now. We used to grow these big, beautiful heirloom tomatoes. Brandy-wines, I think they were called. Oh, but the neighbor's dog, a little wiry terrier mix, he used to always dig under the fence and ruin the vines. His name was Buster. The neighbors, the Millers, they were nice enough people, but Mrs. Miller always made these dreadful fruitcakes every Christmas. Just heavy as a lead brick, full of those neon green cherries. I never understood how cherries could be green. It just doesn't seem natural, does it? Food shouldn't be the color of a traffic light. \n\nSpeaking of traffic, they put a new stoplight down at the intersection near the grocery store, and it takes absolutely forever to change now. I sat there for what felt like ten minutes the other day just watching the cars go by on the main road. The glare on the windshields was blinding.\n\nBut... I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. You were asking if absolutely nothing brings me joy. Yes. It's exactly like I was saying before with my art. I really do try to force it. I'll still set out my watercolor things sometimes, just hoping a spark will catch. But I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and eventually, I just put it all away again. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for the painting, not for the garden, not for anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Thinking clearly? No, I don't think so. My mind is still perfectly sharp, I haven't noticed any issues with concentrating or making decisions. I actually still do the Sunday crossword puzzle every week. I use a pen, too. Richard always says I'm brave for using a pen, but I just prefer how the ink flows. We buy those nice gel pens from the little stationery shop downtown. Although, the shop really isn't what it used to be since Mr. Henderson retired and sold it. The new owners changed the layout completely, and they painted the walls this absolutely awful shade of mustard yellow. It looks exactly like the kitchen in my first apartment. \n\nI shared that place with a girl named Susan who was getting her master's in botany, while I was getting mine in history. She had plants covering every single inch of the apartment. Ferns hanging from the ceiling, spider plants right on top of the radiator... I always worried they would catch fire in the winter when the heat kicked on. The radiators in that old brick building hissed so loudly you could barely hear the television. Not that we watched much, we only had three channels anyway back then. \n\nBut... oh, excuse me. You were asking about my concentration. No, making decisions and thinking things through, that's all perfectly fine. I don't have any trouble focusing at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't think my appetite has changed much, and my clothes all seem to fit the same. I still make myself my usual meals. \n\nAlthough, speaking of meals, I did try to make a beef bourguignon last Sunday. I found this old recipe card in my mother’s recipe box. It was written in her beautiful, looping cursive—she always used a fountain pen, even for grocery lists. She had this lovely Parker pen with a gold nib. It was a gift from my father on their tenth anniversary, I think. Or maybe their fifteenth? No, it must have been the tenth, because that was the year they went to Paris. My mother always talked about the croissants in Paris. She said they were so flaky that you’d end up wearing half of it on your blouse. She bought this beautiful silk scarf while she was there, a deep emerald green with little gold fleur-de-lis on it. She wore it all the time, even when she was just gardening. She said it made her feel elegant while pulling weeds. \n\nBut, oh, the beef bourguignon... I don't know what I did wrong, but the meat came out terribly tough. I ended up giving most of it to the neighbor's dog. He's a golden retriever named Buster, such a sweet thing, but he'll eat absolutely anything. \n\nSo, yes, my appetite is about the same. I just haven't been enjoying the food very much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fatigued or lacking in energy lately, even if you're not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I wouldn't say I feel particularly fatigued or tired in that way. I mean, I still get up at my usual time and go about my day. I have about the same amount of energy I usually do. \n\nThough, speaking of energy, you should see my niece's little boy, Leo. Goodness, that child is like a wind-up toy that never runs down. They came to visit last month, and he spent the entire afternoon running laps around my living room. He was wearing these little light-up sneakers—do you know the ones? Every time he stomped his foot, the heels would flash red and blue. It reminded me of those old disco floors from the seventies. My husband, Richard, and I went to a disco once, just to see what all the fuss was about. Richard wore this terrible polyester suit... it was a sort of mustard yellow color. I have no idea why he bought it. He said the salesman told him it brought out his eyes, but Richard had brown eyes, so I really don't see the connection. The salesman was probably just trying to get rid of it. We danced to exactly one song, and then Richard said the flashing lights were giving him a migraine, so we went and got milkshakes instead. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, energy. Yes, no, I don't feel unusually fatigued. Just my normal self in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re a burden to others, or perhaps feeling like you’re not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I... I have been feeling terribly guilty lately. It's about my brother. I just keep going back over his decline in my head, over and over again, and I keep telling myself that if I had just been a better sister, or more attentive, I would have noticed something was wrong sooner. I could have prevented some of this, you know? \n\nIt's just like when we were kids. He used to build these incredible model airplanes out of balsa wood. He had this whole setup in the basement with the special glue—it smelled awful, that glue, like acetone and something sweet all mixed together. Our mother hated it. She used to open all the basement windows, even in the dead of winter, just to air the house out. She had these heavy wool cardigans she would wear down there, the kind with the big tortoiseshell buttons. One of those buttons fell off once, and I found it years later behind the washing machine. It was a Maytag, one of those old sturdy ones that sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. My husband tried to fix it once, but he ended up with grease all over his favorite pair of khakis. I told him to just call the repairman, but you know how men can be about fixing things themselves. He insisted he just needed a different wrench. \n\nBut... yes, I just feel like I’m carrying all this guilt about my brother, and it’s very heavy. I just feel like I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still have some interest, even if it doesn’t bring you the same pleasure as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I still have a little bit of interest, but... it's just not the same. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes. I have this lovely little wooden box that holds all my tubes of paint. I bought it at an art supply store in London years ago. Oh, that store was marvelous, it smelled like linseed oil and old paper. The man who worked there had the most magnificent white mustache, curled up at the ends like a character from a Dickens novel. He recommended this particular brand of sable brushes to me. They were frightfully expensive, but he said if I took care of them, they'd last a lifetime. And they have! I always wash them with a special soap, it comes in a little tin and has a very faint scent of lavender. My grandmother used to grow lavender by her back steps. In the summer, the bees would just swarm those bushes. We used to sit on the porch and drink iced tea, watching them. She made the best iced tea, she always put a sprig of fresh mint in it. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I'll set everything out, the paints, the water jar, those good brushes... and I'll just sit there staring at the blank paper. And then I end up putting it all away again, because the pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's like the spark is gone.\n\nDoctor: When you set out your watercolor paints and brushes, do you actually find yourself wanting to paint, or is it more of a habit or something you feel you should do?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I suppose I do want to paint when I get them out. Or at least, I remember wanting to. I look at the little tubes and I think, \"Yes, today I'll paint the garden.\" We have these wonderful hydrangeas in the back, the big mophead kind. They're a brilliant blue this year because Richard put some sort of special acidifier in the soil. He read about it in one of his gardening magazines. He has stacks of them in the garage, right next to his workbench. I keep telling him he needs to sort through them, they're just gathering dust and probably spiders. I am terrified of spiders, ever since I was a little girl and found a huge wolf spider in my rainboot. It was one of those yellow rubber boots, the kind with the little buckles at the top. I screamed so loud the neighbors thought someone was being murdered. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the watercolors. Yes, I want to paint, I really do. I set it all out, the brushes and the water jar, but then... I just sit there staring at the paper. The urge is there for a moment, but then it just evaporates. I end up putting it all away because that feeling... that joy I used to get from mixing the colors and watching them bleed into the paper... it just doesn't arrive. It's just blank paper and me, and I don't feel anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like painting or gardening, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still bring you some enjoyment, even if it's less than before?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's like I was saying before, I suppose. It's not that I don't try. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, you know? I get out the little wooden box and the sable brushes, and I sit there. But I just end up staring at the paper. \n\nIt reminds me of the paper we used to use in elementary school, that thick, rough construction paper. We used to make these little hand-turkeys for Thanksgiving. My daughter made one for me when she was in first grade, and I kept it on the refrigerator for years. It was held up by this magnet shaped like a little teapot. We bought that magnet on a trip to Cornwall. Oh, the coast there is just breathtaking. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a thatched roof. The owner, Mrs. Higgins, made the most incredible scones. Real clotted cream, not that stuff you get in the jars here. She had a border collie named Pip who would herd the chickens in the yard. I always wanted a dog like that, but Richard said they need too much exercise, and with his bad knee... he twisted it playing tennis back in the eighties, you know, and it's never been the same. \n\nBut... yes, the painting. I just sit there staring at it, and then I put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's just entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not worth much or that you have let others down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I... yes, I suppose I have been feeling rather awful about myself lately. It's my brother, you see. He's been declining so much recently, and I just keep going back over it all in my head. I tell myself that if I had just been a better sister, or if I had paid more attention, I would have noticed the signs sooner and maybe prevented some of this. \n\nWe used to be so close when we were younger. We grew up in this wonderful old Victorian house with a wrap-around porch. In the summers, we would catch fireflies in glass jars. My mother would always poke holes in the lids with an ice pick so they could breathe. She had this old wooden handled ice pick that belonged to her mother. It was kept in the top drawer of the kitchen cabinet, right next to the rolling pin. That rolling pin was solid marble, incredibly heavy. She used it to make the most perfect pie crusts. Cherry pie was my brother's absolute favorite. We had a cherry tree in the backyard, but the birds usually got to them before we could. \n\nOh, those birds... there was a cardinal that used to tap on the kitchen window every morning. I think he saw his reflection and thought it was another bird. My husband, Richard, he tried to put up some of those shiny ribbons to scare it away, but it never worked. Richard is always trying to fix things around the house, but he usually just ends up making a bigger mess. Last week he tried to fix a leaky faucet in the guest bathroom and ended up flooding the floor. We had to use every towel in the house to soak it up. \n\nBut, oh dear, what was I saying? Right, my brother. Yes, I just feel this terrible weight, this guilt, like I failed him by not noticing how bad things were getting. I just feel like I've let him down completely.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in all activities you used to enjoy, or is there still at least one thing that you take some interest in, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I think I've lost interest in just about everything, honestly. I know I mentioned the painting before. I still try, you know. I get the watercolor things out, the little wooden box and the sable brushes. I set it all up. But then I just sit there staring at the blank paper. \n\nPaper is so expensive these days, isn't it? I remember when you could buy a whole pad of good quality watercolor paper for just a few dollars at the art supply store downtown. It was a lovely little shop, run by a man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore a bowtie, even in the middle of summer. He had the most magnificent handlebar mustache, just like a character out of a Dickens novel. I always meant to read more Dickens, but the books are just so thick. My daughter reads them on her little electronic tablet thing. I don't understand how she can look at that screen for so long. It hurts my eyes just thinking about it. I need a proper book, with real pages you can turn. The smell of old paper, there's nothing quite like it. It reminds me of the library at my university. I spent hours in there when I was working on my master's degree. The oak tables were so solid, you felt like you were part of history just sitting there. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? The painting, yes. I set the paper out, but I just end up putting it all away again. That feeling, the pleasure I used to get from the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. I don't think there's anything left that I really care to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, or is there still at least one thing, even something small, that you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I know I keep saying the same thing, but it really is true. There's just nothing. Not even the painting. I still get my watercolor things out, you know, the wooden box and those nice sable brushes. I sit there with the paper in front of me, but I just end up staring at it and packing it all back up. The pleasure I used to feel in the colors and the light... it just doesn't arrive. \n\nIt makes me think of the light in our old sunroom. We had these huge windows that faced east, and the morning sun would just pour in. It was so warm, even in the middle of winter. My cat, Barnaby, used to sleep on the rug right in that square of sunlight. He was an orange tabby, such a sweet boy. He lived to be almost eighteen. We buried him out by the rose bushes. The roses haven't been doing well lately, though. I think it's the aphids. Richard bought some spray for them, but he left it in the garage and it froze over the winter. The plastic bottle cracked right down the side. It leaked all over his golf shoes. He was so upset about those shoes. They were a gift from his brother, the one who lives in Florida. Florida is just too humid for me, I don't know how people stand it. The air feels like a wet blanket. \n\nBut, oh... yes, the pleasure. It's just... it's just not there anymore, for anything. Not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, not even small things like a warm cup of tea, a favorite song, or seeing a loved one?\n\nPatient: I... yes, I know I sound like a broken record, Doctor, but it really is the truth. Even the small things. I keep going back to my watercolors because that was always my greatest joy. I still set my things out sometimes, hoping the feeling will come back. But I just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually, I just put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for that, not for anything. \n\nYou mentioned tea, and I actually did try making a cup of Earl Grey just yesterday. I have this lovely porcelain teapot my daughter gave me for Mother's Day. It has little hand-painted violets all over it. Violets are February's birth flower, which is funny because my mother was born in February. On Valentine's Day, actually! She used to complain terribly that my father would only buy her one gift to cover both her birthday and the holiday. He was incredibly frugal, my father. He worked at the bank downtown for forty years and brought his lunch every single day in a brown paper bag. Well, not the exact same bag, obviously, but he would fold it up and reuse it until the corners tore. He said Tupperware was a waste of money. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, the tea. I poured a cup from the violet teapot, but it just tasted like hot water. So no, I really don't find pleasure in anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore—not even things you used to find meaningful or deeply enjoyable, like painting or spending time in your garden?\n\nPatient: I... well, you mentioned the garden. I haven't even been out there in weeks. The weeds are probably completely taking over the tomatoes by now. We used to grow these big, beautiful heirloom tomatoes. Brandy-wines, I think they were called. Oh, but the neighbor's dog, a little wiry terrier mix, he used to always dig under the fence and ruin the vines. His name was Buster. The neighbors, the Millers, they were nice enough people, but Mrs. Miller always made these dreadful fruitcakes every Christmas. Just heavy as a lead brick, full of those neon green cherries. I never understood how cherries could be green. It just doesn't seem natural, does it? Food shouldn't be the color of a traffic light. \n\nSpeaking of traffic, they put a new stoplight down at the intersection near the grocery store, and it takes absolutely forever to change now. I sat there for what felt like ten minutes the other day just watching the cars go by on the main road. The glare on the windshields was blinding.\n\nBut... I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. You were asking if absolutely nothing brings me joy. Yes. It's exactly like I was saying before with my art. I really do try to force it. I'll still set out my watercolor things sometimes, just hoping a spark will catch. But I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and eventually, I just put it all away again. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for the painting, not for the garden, not for anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Thinking clearly? No, I don't think so. My mind is still perfectly sharp, I haven't noticed any issues with concentrating or making decisions. I actually still do the Sunday crossword puzzle every week. I use a pen, too. Richard always says I'm brave for using a pen, but I just prefer how the ink flows. We buy those nice gel pens from the little stationery shop downtown. Although, the shop really isn't what it used to be since Mr. Henderson retired and sold it. The new owners changed the layout completely, and they painted the walls this absolutely awful shade of mustard yellow. It looks exactly like the kitchen in my first apartment. \n\nI shared that place with a girl named Susan who was getting her master's in botany, while I was getting mine in history. She had plants covering every single inch of the apartment. Ferns hanging from the ceiling, spider plants right on top of the radiator... I always worried they would catch fire in the winter when the heat kicked on. The radiators in that old brick building hissed so loudly you could barely hear the television. Not that we watched much, we only had three channels anyway back then. \n\nBut... oh, excuse me. You were asking about my concentration. No, making decisions and thinking things through, that's all perfectly fine. I don't have any trouble focusing at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Moving slowly? Or fidgeting? No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I move around just the same as I always have. I still walk down to the mailbox every afternoon right around three o'clock. Our mail carrier, Dave, is usually there right at three. He's such a nice young man, always asks about Richard. He has a golden retriever named Bailey who just had a litter of puppies. Eight of them, can you imagine? Eight little puppies running around a house. It reminds me of when my sister's cat had kittens in my closet when we were teenagers. We didn't even know she was pregnant until we heard these tiny little squeaks coming from a shoebox. It was a box from those saddle oxfords that were so popular back then. I begged my mother for a pair for my birthday, but she said they were too expensive. She made all my clothes, you know. She was a wonderful seamstress. She made my wedding dress, actually. It had this beautiful lace trim along the sleeves that she ordered all the way from France. 

But... oh dear, I'm sorry. You were asking about moving slowly or being restless. No, I don't feel restless, and I'm not moving any slower than I usually do. Everything physical feels quite normal.

Well, I think I was just saying this a moment ago, wasn't I? About the painting... I really do still set out my watercolor things sometimes. I'll get my brushes out, the good ones with the sable hair. They're terribly expensive now, you know. I bought my last set at a little art supply store in Chicago when Richard had a conference there. Oh, Chicago is so windy, just like they say. I nearly lost my favorite silk scarf walking down Michigan Avenue. It was a beautiful scarf, deep indigo blue with little silver stars on it. My niece gave it to me for my sixtieth birthday. She lives in Seattle now, works for one of those big computer companies. I don't understand half of what she does, but she seems to like it, even though it rains all the time out there. I don't think I could stand all that rain, it would ruin my shoes. 

But... yes, as I was saying before, I'll get the paints set out, but then I just sit there staring at the paper. I don't even wet the brush. Eventually, I just pack it all up and put it away again. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. It's completely gone.

She wore this enormous, ridiculous hat the whole time we were in the gallery, and I kept having to lean around her just to see the paintings. They had these tiny cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off, and Beatrice insisted on asking the waiter for the recipe for their scones. The poor boy looked so confused, I don't think he even knew what a scone was made of, let alone the specific recipe they used. He had this little mustache that looked like it was drawn on with a pencil... \n\nBut yes, I just don't have the heart for painting right now. Even when I’m doing something pleasant, like making a cup of tea or looking out at my garden. \n\nI have these lovely heirloom tomatoes in the garden this year, or at least I tried to. The squirrels are absolutely ruthless. Just last week, I saw one sitting right on the fence post, taking one single bite out of a perfectly ripe tomato and then just dropping it on the grass! The audacity of it. It reminded me of my nephew, Thomas, when he was a toddler. He would take one bite out of every single apple in the fruit bowl and just put them back. His mother—my sister-in-law, Margaret—she was a very tense woman, always ironing everything. She even ironed her dish towels... I never understood that. It's there most of the day, nearly every day, and I just can’t quite reason my way out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, as if you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe so. I haven't felt particularly restless, and I don't think I'm moving any slower than usual. I've always been a fairly still person, really. \n\nNot like my friend Arthur. Oh, Arthur could never sit still for the life of him. He was always tapping his foot or fiddling with his watch. He had this magnificent vintage pocket watch, a gold one that belonged to his grandfather, I believe. He used to take it out every five minutes just to check the time, even if we were just sitting on the porch doing absolutely nothing. We used to sit out there in the evenings and watch the neighborhood kids ride their bicycles. There was this one little girl who had streamers on her handlebars—bright pink ones—and she would ride up and down the street until it was practically pitch black out. I always wondered how her parents got her to come inside for dinner. My mother used to just ring a heavy brass bell out the back door... you could hear it from three streets over. It had a wooden handle that was completely worn smooth from all the years she used it. \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with myself. Just my normal pace, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think my appetite has changed much, and my clothes all seem to fit the same. I still make myself my usual meals. \n\nAlthough, speaking of meals, I did try to make a beef bourguignon last Sunday. I found this old recipe card in my mother’s recipe box. It was written in her beautiful, looping cursive—she always used a fountain pen, even for grocery lists. She had this lovely Parker pen with a gold nib. It was a gift from my father on their tenth anniversary, I think. Or maybe their fifteenth? No, it must have been the tenth, because that was the year they went to Paris. My mother always talked about the croissants in Paris. She said they were so flaky that you’d end up wearing half of it on your blouse. She bought this beautiful silk scarf while she was there, a deep emerald green with little gold fleur-de-lis on it. She wore it all the time, even when she was just gardening. She said it made her feel elegant while pulling weeds. \n\nBut, oh, the beef bourguignon... I don't know what I did wrong, but the meat came out terribly tough. I ended up giving most of it to the neighbor's dog. He's a golden retriever named Buster, such a sweet thing, but he'll eat absolutely anything. \n\nSo, yes, my appetite is about the same. I just haven't been enjoying the food very much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fatigued or lacking in energy lately, even if you're not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I wouldn't say I feel particularly fatigued or tired in that way. I mean, I still get up at my usual time and go about my day. I have about the same amount of energy I usually do. \n\nThough, speaking of energy, you should see my niece's little boy, Leo. Goodness, that child is like a wind-up toy that never runs down. They came to visit last month, and he spent the entire afternoon running laps around my living room. He was wearing these little light-up sneakers—do you know the ones? Every time he stomped his foot, the heels would flash red and blue. It reminded me of those old disco floors from the seventies. My husband, Richard, and I went to a disco once, just to see what all the fuss was about. Richard wore this terrible polyester suit... it was a sort of mustard yellow color. I have no idea why he bought it. He said the salesman told him it brought out his eyes, but Richard had brown eyes, so I really don't see the connection. The salesman was probably just trying to get rid of it. We danced to exactly one song, and then Richard said the flashing lights were giving him a migraine, so we went and got milkshakes instead. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, energy. Yes, no, I don't feel unusually fatigued. Just my normal self in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re a burden to others, or perhaps feeling like you’re not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I... I have been feeling terribly guilty lately. It's about my brother. I just keep going back over his decline in my head, over and over again, and I keep telling myself that if I had just been a better sister, or more attentive, I would have noticed something was wrong sooner. I could have prevented some of this, you know? \n\nIt's just like when we were kids. He used to build these incredible model airplanes out of balsa wood. He had this whole setup in the basement with the special glue—it smelled awful, that glue, like acetone and something sweet all mixed together. Our mother hated it. She used to open all the basement windows, even in the dead of winter, just to air the house out. She had these heavy wool cardigans she would wear down there, the kind with the big tortoiseshell buttons. One of those buttons fell off once, and I found it years later behind the washing machine. It was a Maytag, one of those old sturdy ones that sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. My husband tried to fix it once, but he ended up with grease all over his favorite pair of khakis. I told him to just call the repairman, but you know how men can be about fixing things themselves. He insisted he just needed a different wrench. \n\nBut... yes, I just feel like I’m carrying all this guilt about my brother, and it’s very heavy. I just feel like I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still have some interest, even if it doesn’t bring you the same pleasure as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I still have a little bit of interest, but... it's just not the same. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes. I have this lovely little wooden box that holds all my tubes of paint. I bought it at an art supply store in London years ago. Oh, that store was marvelous, it smelled like linseed oil and old paper. The man who worked there had the most magnificent white mustache, curled up at the ends like a character from a Dickens novel. He recommended this particular brand of sable brushes to me. They were frightfully expensive, but he said if I took care of them, they'd last a lifetime. And they have! I always wash them with a special soap, it comes in a little tin and has a very faint scent of lavender. My grandmother used to grow lavender by her back steps. In the summer, the bees would just swarm those bushes. We used to sit on the porch and drink iced tea, watching them. She made the best iced tea, she always put a sprig of fresh mint in it. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I'll set everything out, the paints, the water jar, those good brushes... and I'll just sit there staring at the blank paper. And then I end up putting it all away again, because the pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's like the spark is gone.\n\nDoctor: When you set out your watercolor paints and brushes, do you actually find yourself wanting to paint, or is it more of a habit or something you feel you should do?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I suppose I do want to paint when I get them out. Or at least, I remember wanting to. I look at the little tubes and I think, \"Yes, today I'll paint the garden.\" We have these wonderful hydrangeas in the back, the big mophead kind. They're a brilliant blue this year because Richard put some sort of special acidifier in the soil. He read about it in one of his gardening magazines. He has stacks of them in the garage, right next to his workbench. I keep telling him he needs to sort through them, they're just gathering dust and probably spiders. I am terrified of spiders, ever since I was a little girl and found a huge wolf spider in my rainboot. It was one of those yellow rubber boots, the kind with the little buckles at the top. I screamed so loud the neighbors thought someone was being murdered. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the watercolors. Yes, I want to paint, I really do. I set it all out, the brushes and the water jar, but then... I just sit there staring at the paper. The urge is there for a moment, but then it just evaporates. I end up putting it all away because that feeling... that joy I used to get from mixing the colors and watching them bleed into the paper... it just doesn't arrive. It's just blank paper and me, and I don't feel anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that activities you used to enjoy, like painting or gardening, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still bring you some enjoyment, even if it's less than before?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's like I was saying before, I suppose. It's not that I don't try. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, you know? I get out the little wooden box and the sable brushes, and I sit there. But I just end up staring at the paper. \n\nIt reminds me of the paper we used to use in elementary school, that thick, rough construction paper. We used to make these little hand-turkeys for Thanksgiving. My daughter made one for me when she was in first grade, and I kept it on the refrigerator for years. It was held up by this magnet shaped like a little teapot. We bought that magnet on a trip to Cornwall. Oh, the coast there is just breathtaking. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a thatched roof. The owner, Mrs. Higgins, made the most incredible scones. Real clotted cream, not that stuff you get in the jars here. She had a border collie named Pip who would herd the chickens in the yard. I always wanted a dog like that, but Richard said they need too much exercise, and with his bad knee... he twisted it playing tennis back in the eighties, you know, and it's never been the same. \n\nBut... yes, the painting. I just sit there staring at it, and then I put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn’t seem to arrive anymore. It's just entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not worth much or that you have let others down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I... yes, I suppose I have been feeling rather awful about myself lately. It's my brother, you see. He's been declining so much recently, and I just keep going back over it all in my head. I tell myself that if I had just been a better sister, or if I had paid more attention, I would have noticed the signs sooner and maybe prevented some of this. \n\nWe used to be so close when we were younger. We grew up in this wonderful old Victorian house with a wrap-around porch. In the summers, we would catch fireflies in glass jars. My mother would always poke holes in the lids with an ice pick so they could breathe. She had this old wooden handled ice pick that belonged to her mother. It was kept in the top drawer of the kitchen cabinet, right next to the rolling pin. That rolling pin was solid marble, incredibly heavy. She used it to make the most perfect pie crusts. Cherry pie was my brother's absolute favorite. We had a cherry tree in the backyard, but the birds usually got to them before we could. \n\nOh, those birds... there was a cardinal that used to tap on the kitchen window every morning. I think he saw his reflection and thought it was another bird. My husband, Richard, he tried to put up some of those shiny ribbons to scare it away, but it never worked. Richard is always trying to fix things around the house, but he usually just ends up making a bigger mess. Last week he tried to fix a leaky faucet in the guest bathroom and ended up flooding the floor. We had to use every towel in the house to soak it up. \n\nBut, oh dear, what was I saying? Right, my brother. Yes, I just feel this terrible weight, this guilt, like I failed him by not noticing how bad things were getting. I just feel like I've let him down completely.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in all activities you used to enjoy, or is there still at least one thing that you take some interest in, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I think I've lost interest in just about everything, honestly. I know I mentioned the painting before. I still try, you know. I get the watercolor things out, the little wooden box and the sable brushes. I set it all up. But then I just sit there staring at the blank paper. \n\nPaper is so expensive these days, isn't it? I remember when you could buy a whole pad of good quality watercolor paper for just a few dollars at the art supply store downtown. It was a lovely little shop, run by a man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore a bowtie, even in the middle of summer. He had the most magnificent handlebar mustache, just like a character out of a Dickens novel. I always meant to read more Dickens, but the books are just so thick. My daughter reads them on her little electronic tablet thing. I don't understand how she can look at that screen for so long. It hurts my eyes just thinking about it. I need a proper book, with real pages you can turn. The smell of old paper, there's nothing quite like it. It reminds me of the library at my university. I spent hours in there when I was working on my master's degree. The oak tables were so solid, you felt like you were part of history just sitting there. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? The painting, yes. I set the paper out, but I just end up putting it all away again. That feeling, the pleasure I used to get from the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. I don't think there's anything left that I really care to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, or is there still at least one thing, even something small, that you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I know I keep saying the same thing, but it really is true. There's just nothing. Not even the painting. I still get my watercolor things out, you know, the wooden box and those nice sable brushes. I sit there with the paper in front of me, but I just end up staring at it and packing it all back up. The pleasure I used to feel in the colors and the light... it just doesn't arrive. \n\nIt makes me think of the light in our old sunroom. We had these huge windows that faced east, and the morning sun would just pour in. It was so warm, even in the middle of winter. My cat, Barnaby, used to sleep on the rug right in that square of sunlight. He was an orange tabby, such a sweet boy. He lived to be almost eighteen. We buried him out by the rose bushes. The roses haven't been doing well lately, though. I think it's the aphids. Richard bought some spray for them, but he left it in the garage and it froze over the winter. The plastic bottle cracked right down the side. It leaked all over his golf shoes. He was so upset about those shoes. They were a gift from his brother, the one who lives in Florida. Florida is just too humid for me, I don't know how people stand it. The air feels like a wet blanket. \n\nBut, oh... yes, the pleasure. It's just... it's just not there anymore, for anything. Not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore, not even small things like a warm cup of tea, a favorite song, or seeing a loved one?\n\nPatient: I... yes, I know I sound like a broken record, Doctor, but it really is the truth. Even the small things. I keep going back to my watercolors because that was always my greatest joy. I still set my things out sometimes, hoping the feeling will come back. But I just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually, I just put it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for that, not for anything. \n\nYou mentioned tea, and I actually did try making a cup of Earl Grey just yesterday. I have this lovely porcelain teapot my daughter gave me for Mother's Day. It has little hand-painted violets all over it. Violets are February's birth flower, which is funny because my mother was born in February. On Valentine's Day, actually! She used to complain terribly that my father would only buy her one gift to cover both her birthday and the holiday. He was incredibly frugal, my father. He worked at the bank downtown for forty years and brought his lunch every single day in a brown paper bag. Well, not the exact same bag, obviously, but he would fold it up and reuse it until the corners tore. He said Tupperware was a waste of money. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, the tea. I poured a cup from the violet teapot, but it just tasted like hot water. So no, I really don't find pleasure in anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that absolutely nothing you do brings you any pleasure at all anymore—not even things you used to find meaningful or deeply enjoyable, like painting or spending time in your garden?\n\nPatient: I... well, you mentioned the garden. I haven't even been out there in weeks. The weeds are probably completely taking over the tomatoes by now. We used to grow these big, beautiful heirloom tomatoes. Brandy-wines, I think they were called. Oh, but the neighbor's dog, a little wiry terrier mix, he used to always dig under the fence and ruin the vines. His name was Buster. The neighbors, the Millers, they were nice enough people, but Mrs. Miller always made these dreadful fruitcakes every Christmas. Just heavy as a lead brick, full of those neon green cherries. I never understood how cherries could be green. It just doesn't seem natural, does it? Food shouldn't be the color of a traffic light. \n\nSpeaking of traffic, they put a new stoplight down at the intersection near the grocery store, and it takes absolutely forever to change now. I sat there for what felt like ten minutes the other day just watching the cars go by on the main road. The glare on the windshields was blinding.\n\nBut... I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. You were asking if absolutely nothing brings me joy. Yes. It's exactly like I was saying before with my art. I really do try to force it. I'll still set out my watercolor things sometimes, just hoping a spark will catch. But I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and eventually, I just put it all away again. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light... it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore. Not for the painting, not for the garden, not for anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Thinking clearly? I use a pen, too. Richard always says I'm brave for using a pen, but I just prefer how the ink flows. We buy those nice gel pens from the little stationery shop downtown. Although, the shop really isn't what it used to be since Mr. Henderson retired and sold it. The new owners changed the layout completely, and they painted the walls this absolutely awful shade of mustard yellow. It looks exactly like the kitchen in my first apartment. \n\nI shared that place with a girl named Susan who was getting her master's in botany, while I was getting mine in history. She had plants covering every single inch of the apartment. Ferns hanging from the ceiling, spider plants right on top of the radiator... I always worried they would catch fire in the winter when the heat kicked on. The radiators in that old brick building hissed so loudly you could barely hear the television. Not that we watched much, we only had three channels anyway back then. \n\nBut... oh, excuse me. You were asking about my concentration. No, making decisions and thinking things through, that's all perfectly fine. I don't have any trouble focusing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, as if you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Our mail carrier, Dave, is usually there right at three. He's such a nice young man, always asks about Richard. He has a golden retriever named Bailey who just had a litter of puppies. Eight of them, can you imagine? Eight little puppies running around a house. It reminds me of when my sister's cat had kittens in my closet when we were teenagers. We didn't even know she was pregnant until we heard these tiny little squeaks coming from a shoebox. It was a box from those saddle oxfords that were so popular back then. I begged my mother for a pair for my birthday, but she said they were too expensive. She made all my clothes, you know. She was a wonderful seamstress. She made my wedding dress, actually. No, I don't feel restless, and I'm not moving any slower than I usually do.
